========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m honestly not sure I can give you a solid answer to that. Like, I know you’re asking if it’s been the majority of the past two years, and I feel like I should be able to look back and say yes or no, but it gets blurry for me.

I can say there have definitely been long stretches where those things didn’t feel the way they used to. Basketball, dance nights, mentoring — I still know what they were supposed to mean to me, but the actual interest or pleasure hasn’t been easy to access. But whether that counts as “most of the things” and “majority of the time,” I don’t know. I don’t really track it that clearly.

It’s more like I notice it when I’m supposed to want to do something and I just… don’t feel much. Or I think, “I used to be the kind of person who would care about this more.” But I can’t confidently put a percentage on it. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not the clean answer you’re looking for.

And guilt—I don’t know. I’ll feel bad about little things sometimes, like if I didn’t do something the way I thought I should, but I’m not sure it’s this constant guilt that doesn’t make sense logically.\n\nI guess I haven’t really tracked it that way. I mean, I have moments where I’m kind of foggy or I’ll sit there longer than I probably should before doing something, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as not being able to think clearly or concentrate almost every day.\n\nWith decisions, I can overthink things sometimes, but I don’t know that it feels like a separate problem, if that makes sense. Like, if I’m picking something or trying to decide what to do next, sometimes I just feel stuck, but I don’t really know whether that’s because I can’t decide or because I just don’t feel very motivated in that moment.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a way where I’d confidently say yes, it’s hard to concentrate or make decisions almost every day. It started around when I had to stop taking my community college classes because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule work together. That was kind of the point where things shifted, and since then it hasn’t really gone away in any big, lasting way. Or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nIt’s also started affecting work, which makes me feel embarrassed to say. Have you noticed that, even when you do those things now, they don’t bring you the same sense of enjoyment or satisfaction as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m trying to think of it the way you’re asking, and I’m not sure I can say for sure.\n\nLike, I can remember things I used to like, and I still do some of them sometimes, but I don’t always pay attention to whether the enjoyment is “less” or if I’m just sort of… not in the right headspace. I guess sometimes it feels kind of muted, like I’m there but not fully taking it in. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as those things don’t bring me satisfaction anymore.\n\nIt’s hard because I’ve been feeling low for so long that it’s almost like I don’t have a clean before-and-after comparison. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I think I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed that exact thing, at least not in a way I could explain confidently.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy almost every day, even if you haven’t done anything physically or mentally exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice the most day to day. It’s not even like I have to do something huge to feel wiped out. Even small, normal things feel like they take way more energy than they should.\n\nLike at work, filling out inspection logs shouldn’t be this big task, but sometimes it feels like I’m dragging myself through it, like my brain and body are both just heavy. Or if I’m supposed to drive to basketball, even just getting myself together and getting in the car feels like this whole mountain. And washing my hair sounds so basic, but there are days where I’ll put it off because the thought of doing all the steps feels like more energy than I have.\n\nAnd it’s not really fixed by resting, which is frustrating. On my days off I’ll think, okay, I’ll sleep in or just do nothing for a while and then maybe I’ll feel recharged, but I don’t really feel refilled. It’s more like I’m just less active, but still tired underneath.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty about things more often than not, even when it doesn’t seem to make sense logically?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know I’m hard on myself sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I feel worthless, exactly, or guilty more often than not.\n\nIt’s more like… I can second-guess myself a lot or feel like I should be doing better somehow, but when you say “worthless,” that feels like a really strong word, and I don’t know if that’s what I’m feeling. And guilt—I don’t know. I’ll feel bad about little things sometimes, like if I didn’t do something the way I thought I should, but I’m not sure it’s this constant guilt that doesn’t make sense logically.\n\nI guess I haven’t really tracked it that way. I notice being down, but I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that specific feeling of being worthless or guilty most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions, even with simple things, almost every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that clearly. I mean, I have moments where I’m kind of foggy or I’ll sit there longer than I probably should before doing something, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as not being able to think clearly or concentrate almost every day.\n\nWith decisions, I can overthink things sometimes, but I don’t know that it feels like a separate problem, if that makes sense. Like, if I’m picking something or trying to decide what to do next, sometimes I just feel stuck, but I don’t really know whether that’s because I can’t decide or because I just don’t feel very motivated in that moment.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a way where I’d confidently say yes, it’s hard to concentrate or make decisions almost every day. It’s more like there are times I feel mentally slowed down, but I’m not sure how often or if it’s enough to describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times in the past when you felt down or depressed, and then had a period where you felt significantly better for several months or more before feeling this way again?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve had better days or maybe even a better couple weeks here and there, but I don’t think I’ve had a stretch of several months where I felt significantly better and then it came back again.\n\nIt feels more like this has been pretty continuous for about two years and four months now. It started around when I had to stop taking my community college classes because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule work together. That was kind of the point where things shifted, and since then it hasn’t really gone away in any big, lasting way. Like I said, there are days that are less bad, but I wouldn’t describe it as me being back to normal for months or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about or look forward to, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard for me to say it like, “I don’t care about anything,” because I do care, or at least I know I used to care. But I’ve mostly stopped actually going to things I used to look forward to.\n\nLike basketball—I used to show up for that pretty regularly, and dance nights too. Those were things where even if I was tired, I’d usually still feel like it was worth going. And the church STEM mentoring, I used to really care about that, like helping the younger kids and feeling like I was doing something useful. But lately I just… don’t go. Or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nIt’s also started affecting work, which makes me feel embarrassed to say. I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve been missing shifts, and when I do go, I’m turning in my inspection documentation late. I’m an inspector, so the documentation is part of the job, it’s not like some optional thing. I know that. But it just piles up, and then I avoid it, and then it’s late.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest, or maybe the follow-through on caring. I don’t know if that makes sense, but things I used to be connected to have kind of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you feel restless or unable to sit still, almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything obvious like people telling me I’m talking slower, or that I’m moving differently. I mean, sometimes I feel heavy or kind of like it takes more effort to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as my movements actually being slowed down.\n\nAnd with restlessness… I don’t think I’m pacing all the time or unable to sit still every day. Sometimes I shift around or feel uncomfortable, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking. I guess if someone else watched me maybe they’d notice something I don’t, but from my side, I wouldn’t confidently say yes to either of those almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby you used to enjoy before this low mood started—something you looked forward to—and tell me how you feel about it now? Do you still get pleasure from it, or does it feel like that enjoyment is gone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… basketball is probably the clearest one. I used to really look forward to it. Like, even if I had work or I was tired, I’d still kind of have that feeling of, “Okay, at least I get to go play,” or just be around people and move around and not be in my head so much. Same with dance nights, honestly. I wasn’t amazing or anything, but it was fun, and it felt like something that was mine.\n\nNow it’s more like… I’ll think about going, and then it just feels like too much effort, or like I already know I’m not going to enjoy it the same way, so I don’t go. I’ve mostly stopped showing up for basketball and dance nights. And the church STEM mentoring too—that one makes me feel really guilty, because I did care about it. I liked helping the kids and feeling useful. But now I keep missing it or putting it off, and then I feel worse because I didn’t go.\n\nIt’s not that I hate those things now. It’s more like the part of me that used to get something out of them is kind of muted or gone. I can remember enjoying them, but actually going and feeling that enjoyment now doesn’t really happen much. And it’s spilled into work too, which is embarrassing—I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve been missing shifts and turning in my inspection documentation late. So it’s not just hobbies being less fun, it’s like I’m not following through on things I used to be able to do.\n\nDoctor: Can you say that your low mood has been present most of the day, nearly every day, for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I can say that. It’s been about two years and four months now, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started around when I had to stop taking my community-college classes because of money and my work schedule. I remember that pretty clearly because it felt like this big shift, like I was trying to make it work and then I just couldn’t, and after that my mood kind of settled into this low place and never really went back to normal. It’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but most days, for most of the day, it’s there. \n\nSo yes—at least two years. A little over two years, actually.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely stopped getting any pleasure or satisfaction from activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, or does it feel like there’s still some part of you that knows they’re good, even if you don’t follow through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely gone, like zero pleasure across the board. That feels too absolute, maybe. It’s more like… I can still recognize that those things matter to me, or mattered to me. Like with basketball, I know on some level it’s good for me, and I know I used to like the feeling of playing and being around people. With mentoring, I still know it’s a good thing, and I still like the idea of being helpful.\n\nBut the actual feeling of looking forward to it, or feeling satisfied afterward, is just really dulled down. Sometimes I’ll tell myself, “You should go, you might feel better,” but it doesn’t really connect emotionally. It’s like I know it in my head but I don’t feel it in my body, if that makes sense.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped getting any pleasure ever. It’s more that the pleasure is faint or unreliable, and most of the time the effort of doing it feels bigger than whatever good feeling I might get out of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, for most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think so, but I’m not sure how to measure “most of the time” exactly. It hasn’t been like I woke up one day and suddenly nothing mattered. It’s more like, over time, those things just became harder to feel connected to.\n\nWith basketball or dance nights, I can still remember that I used to enjoy them, and sometimes I even think maybe I should go because it might be good for me. But most of the time, the actual interest or excitement isn’t really there in the way it used to be. It feels kind of far away, like I’m remembering someone else being into those things.\n\nAnd with mentoring, it’s a little different because I still think it’s meaningful, so I don’t want to say I don’t care at all. I do care, at least in my head. But the motivation and the good feeling I used to get from showing up… that’s been really inconsistent, or kind of dulled. So, yeah, I’d say for a lot of the past couple years, I haven’t really been enjoying those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, for most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I can still remember liking them. Sometimes I’ll even think, “Maybe I should do that again,” but then when it comes time to actually go or make plans, it feels kind of flat. Not always completely empty, just not enough pull to get me moving.\n\nSo I guess I’d say there has been a loss, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it’s been constant or every day. It’s more like the interest comes through really faintly sometimes, and then disappears again. Most of the time it feels dulled or distant, but not totally gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, over the past two years, you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in most of the things you used to enjoy — like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring — for the majority of that time?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can give you a solid answer to that. Basketball, dance nights, mentoring — I still know what they were supposed to mean to me, but the actual interest or pleasure hasn’t been easy to access. But whether that counts as “most of the things” and “majority of the time,” I don’t know. I don’t really track it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I notice it when I’m supposed to want to do something and I just… don’t feel much. Or I think, “I used to be the kind of person who would care about this more.” But I can’t confidently put a percentage on it. I think… I still get stuck on the “most of them” part. Like I can tell you there are things that don’t feel as good anymore, and there are plenty of times where I feel kind of disconnected from things I used to be more excited about. But when you ask if it’s most things, for the majority of the last two years, I don’t know if I can honestly say that with confidence.

So… I guess the most accurate answer is that I’m not sure. I’ve noticed a change, definitely, but I can’t clearly say it’s been most activities for most of the time.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.2, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I’ve just been feeling really low for a long time. It’s not like a bad week or something that passes. It’s been the last couple years, and it’s kind of just always there.\n\nEven when I’m at work, or at church, or with people I care about, I still have this heavy, empty feeling. Like I can be in the room and doing what I’m supposed to do, talking to people, getting through things, but inside I just feel… down. It doesn’t really lift, or if it does, it’s not for very long. I’m tired of feeling like this all the time, so I thought I should finally come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling down for a couple of years—can you clarify if there have been times when you felt this way almost every day, and if so, how long those periods lasted?\n\nPatient: Yeah… almost every day, I’d say. It’s hard to separate it out because it’s been so constant, but when I think back, it really started around when I had to stop taking community-college classes. That was because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule fit anymore, and… I don’t know, it just felt like something I was trying to build got cut off.\n\nThat was about two years and four months ago. Since then, it’s not like I’ve had a long stretch where I felt like myself again. Maybe little moments here and there, but the low feeling has been there pretty much day after day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable, like hobbies, time with friends, or other things that mattered to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I still do some of those things, like I’ll still go places or be around people sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more like everything feels colored by the low mood, if that makes sense. So if I’m doing something that should be nice, part of me can recognize that it’s nice, but I’m also kind of… not fully in it. But I don’t know if that means I stopped enjoying things, or if I’m just distracted by how I’ve been feeling. I haven’t really kept track of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back to a time when you used to enjoy certain activities, like hobbies or time with friends? Have you noticed that, even when you do those things now, they don’t bring you the same sense of enjoyment or satisfaction as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m trying to think of it the way you’re asking, and I’m not sure I can say for sure.\n\nLike, I can remember things I used to like, and I still do some of them sometimes, but I don’t always pay attention to whether the enjoyment is “less” or if I’m just sort of… not in the right headspace. I guess sometimes it feels kind of muted, like I’m there but not fully taking it in. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as those things don’t bring me satisfaction anymore.\n\nIt’s hard because I’ve been feeling low for so long that it’s almost like I don’t have a clean before-and-after comparison. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I think I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed that exact thing, at least not in a way I could explain confidently.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy almost every day, even if you haven’t done anything physically or mentally exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice the most day to day. It’s not even like I have to do something huge to feel wiped out. Even small, normal things feel like they take way more energy than they should.\n\nLike at work, filling out inspection logs shouldn’t be this big task, but sometimes it feels like I’m dragging myself through it, like my brain and body are both just heavy. Or if I’m supposed to drive to basketball, even just getting myself together and getting in the car feels like this whole mountain. And washing my hair sounds so basic, but there are days where I’ll put it off because the thought of doing all the steps feels like more energy than I have.\n\nAnd it’s not really fixed by resting, which is frustrating. On my days off I’ll think, okay, I’ll sleep in or just do nothing for a while and then maybe I’ll feel recharged, but I don’t really feel refilled. It’s more like I’m just less active, but still tired underneath.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty about things more often than not, even when it doesn’t seem to make sense logically?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know I’m hard on myself sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I feel worthless, exactly, or guilty more often than not.\n\nIt’s more like… I can second-guess myself a lot or feel like I should be doing better somehow, but when you say “worthless,” that feels like a really strong word, and I don’t know if that’s what I’m feeling. And guilt—I don’t know. I’ll feel bad about little things sometimes, like if I didn’t do something the way I thought I should, but I’m not sure it’s this constant guilt that doesn’t make sense logically.\n\nI guess I haven’t really tracked it that way. I notice being down, but I don’t know that I’ve clearly noticed that specific feeling of being worthless or guilty most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions, even with simple things, almost every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that clearly. I mean, I have moments where I’m kind of foggy or I’ll sit there longer than I probably should before doing something, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as not being able to think clearly or concentrate almost every day.\n\nWith decisions, I can overthink things sometimes, but I don’t know that it feels like a separate problem, if that makes sense. Like, if I’m picking something or trying to decide what to do next, sometimes I just feel stuck, but I don’t really know whether that’s because I can’t decide or because I just don’t feel very motivated in that moment.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a way where I’d confidently say yes, it’s hard to concentrate or make decisions almost every day. It’s more like there are times I feel mentally slowed down, but I’m not sure how often or if it’s enough to describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times in the past when you felt down or depressed, and then had a period where you felt significantly better for several months or more before feeling this way again?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve had better days or maybe even a better couple weeks here and there, but I don’t think I’ve had a stretch of several months where I felt significantly better and then it came back again.\n\nIt feels more like this has been pretty continuous for about two years and four months now. It started around when I had to stop taking my community college classes because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule work together. That was kind of the point where things shifted, and since then it hasn’t really gone away in any big, lasting way. Like I said, there are days that are less bad, but I wouldn’t describe it as me being back to normal for months or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about or look forward to, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard for me to say it like, “I don’t care about anything,” because I do care, or at least I know I used to care. But I’ve mostly stopped actually going to things I used to look forward to.\n\nLike basketball—I used to show up for that pretty regularly, and dance nights too. Those were things where even if I was tired, I’d usually still feel like it was worth going. And the church STEM mentoring, I used to really care about that, like helping the younger kids and feeling like I was doing something useful. But lately I just… don’t go. Or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nIt’s also started affecting work, which makes me feel embarrassed to say. I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve been missing shifts, and when I do go, I’m turning in my inspection documentation late. I’m an inspector, so the documentation is part of the job, it’s not like some optional thing. I know that. But it just piles up, and then I avoid it, and then it’s late.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest, or maybe the follow-through on caring. I don’t know if that makes sense, but things I used to be connected to have kind of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you feel restless or unable to sit still, almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything obvious like people telling me I’m talking slower, or that I’m moving differently. I mean, sometimes I feel heavy or kind of like it takes more effort to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as my movements actually being slowed down.\n\nAnd with restlessness… I don’t think I’m pacing all the time or unable to sit still every day. Sometimes I shift around or feel uncomfortable, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking. I guess if someone else watched me maybe they’d notice something I don’t, but from my side, I wouldn’t confidently say yes to either of those almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby you used to enjoy before this low mood started—something you looked forward to—and tell me how you feel about it now? Do you still get pleasure from it, or does it feel like that enjoyment is gone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… basketball is probably the clearest one. I used to really look forward to it. Like, even if I had work or I was tired, I’d still kind of have that feeling of, “Okay, at least I get to go play,” or just be around people and move around and not be in my head so much. Same with dance nights, honestly. I wasn’t amazing or anything, but it was fun, and it felt like something that was mine.\n\nNow it’s more like… I’ll think about going, and then it just feels like too much effort, or like I already know I’m not going to enjoy it the same way, so I don’t go. I’ve mostly stopped showing up for basketball and dance nights. And the church STEM mentoring too—that one makes me feel really guilty, because I did care about it. I liked helping the kids and feeling useful. But now I keep missing it or putting it off, and then I feel worse because I didn’t go.\n\nIt’s not that I hate those things now. It’s more like the part of me that used to get something out of them is kind of muted or gone. I can remember enjoying them, but actually going and feeling that enjoyment now doesn’t really happen much. And it’s spilled into work too, which is embarrassing—I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve been missing shifts and turning in my inspection documentation late. So it’s not just hobbies being less fun, it’s like I’m not following through on things I used to be able to do.\n\nDoctor: Can you say that your low mood has been present most of the day, nearly every day, for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I can say that. It’s been about two years and four months now, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started around when I had to stop taking my community-college classes because of money and my work schedule. I remember that pretty clearly because it felt like this big shift, like I was trying to make it work and then I just couldn’t, and after that my mood kind of settled into this low place and never really went back to normal. It’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but most days, for most of the day, it’s there. \n\nSo yes—at least two years. A little over two years, actually.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely stopped getting any pleasure or satisfaction from activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, or does it feel like there’s still some part of you that knows they’re good, even if you don’t follow through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely gone, like zero pleasure across the board. That feels too absolute, maybe. It’s more like… I can still recognize that those things matter to me, or mattered to me. Like with basketball, I know on some level it’s good for me, and I know I used to like the feeling of playing and being around people. With mentoring, I still know it’s a good thing, and I still like the idea of being helpful.\n\nBut the actual feeling of looking forward to it, or feeling satisfied afterward, is just really dulled down. Sometimes I’ll tell myself, “You should go, you might feel better,” but it doesn’t really connect emotionally. It’s like I know it in my head but I don’t feel it in my body, if that makes sense.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped getting any pleasure ever. It’s more that the pleasure is faint or unreliable, and most of the time the effort of doing it feels bigger than whatever good feeling I might get out of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, for most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think so, but I’m not sure how to measure “most of the time” exactly. It hasn’t been like I woke up one day and suddenly nothing mattered. It’s more like, over time, those things just became harder to feel connected to.\n\nWith basketball or dance nights, I can still remember that I used to enjoy them, and sometimes I even think maybe I should go because it might be good for me. But most of the time, the actual interest or excitement isn’t really there in the way it used to be. It feels kind of far away, like I’m remembering someone else being into those things.\n\nAnd with mentoring, it’s a little different because I still think it’s meaningful, so I don’t want to say I don’t care at all. I do care, at least in my head. But the motivation and the good feeling I used to get from showing up… that’s been really inconsistent, or kind of dulled. So, yeah, I’d say for a lot of the past couple years, I haven’t really been enjoying those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, for most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I know you’re asking “most of the time,” and I get what you mean, but it’s hard for me to separate actual loss of interest from just… not following through, or being tired of the whole process of deciding.\n\nLike, with things I used to enjoy, I can still understand that they’re supposed to be good. I can still remember liking them. Sometimes I’ll even think, “Maybe I should do that again,” but then when it comes time to actually go or make plans, it feels kind of flat. Not always completely empty, just not enough pull to get me moving.\n\nSo I guess I’d say there has been a loss, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it’s been constant or every day. It’s more like the interest comes through really faintly sometimes, and then disappears again. Most of the time it feels dulled or distant, but not totally gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, over the past two years, you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in most of the things you used to enjoy — like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring — for the majority of that time?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can give you a solid answer to that. Like, I know you’re asking if it’s been the majority of the past two years, and I feel like I should be able to look back and say yes or no, but it gets blurry for me.\n\nI can say there have definitely been long stretches where those things didn’t feel the way they used to. Basketball, dance nights, mentoring — I still know what they were supposed to mean to me, but the actual interest or pleasure hasn’t been easy to access. But whether that counts as “most of the things” and “majority of the time,” I don’t know. I don’t really track it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I notice it when I’m supposed to want to do something and I just… don’t feel much. Or I think, “I used to be the kind of person who would care about this more.” But I can’t confidently put a percentage on it. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not the clean answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that over the past two years, you’ve generally lost interest or stopped getting pleasure from most of the things you used to enjoy — not just a few activities, but most of them — for the majority of that time?\n\nPatient: I think… I still get stuck on the “most of them” part. Like I can tell you there are things that don’t feel as good anymore, and there are plenty of times where I feel kind of disconnected from things I used to be more excited about. But when you ask if it’s most things, for the majority of the last two years, I don’t know if I can honestly say that with confidence.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to avoid the question. I just don’t really have a clear mental record of it. Some things I still do because I know they matter, or because I think I should, but I don’t always know if that means I’ve lost pleasure in them or if I’m just not as engaged as I used to be. And then other times I might enjoy something a little, but it feels muted, so I don’t know whether that counts as enjoying it or not.\n\nSo… I guess the most accurate answer is that I’m not sure. I’ve noticed a change, definitely, but I can’t clearly say it’s been most activities for most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s been about two years and four months now, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started around when I had to stop taking my community-college classes because of money and my work schedule. I remember that pretty clearly because it felt like this big shift, like I was trying to make it work and then I just couldn’t, and after that my mood kind of settled into this low place and never really went back to normal. It’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but most days, for most of the day, it’s there. \n\nSo yes—at least two years. A little over two years, actually.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely stopped getting any pleasure or satisfaction from activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, or does it feel like there’s still some part of you that knows they’re good, even if you don’t follow through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely gone, like zero pleasure across the board. That feels too absolute, maybe. It’s more like… I can still recognize that those things matter to me, or mattered to me. Like with basketball, I know on some level it’s good for me, and I know I used to like the feeling of playing and being around people. With mentoring, I still know it’s a good thing, and I still like the idea of being helpful.\n\nBut the actual feeling of looking forward to it, or feeling satisfied afterward, is just really dulled down. Sometimes I’ll tell myself, “You should go, you might feel better,” but it doesn’t really connect emotionally. It’s like I know it in my head but I don’t feel it in my body, if that makes sense.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped getting any pleasure ever. It’s more that the pleasure is faint or unreliable, and most of the time the effort of doing it feels bigger than whatever good feeling I might get out of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, for most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think so, but I’m not sure how to measure “most of the time” exactly. It hasn’t been like I woke up one day and suddenly nothing mattered. It’s more like, over time, those things just became harder to feel connected to.\n\nWith basketball or dance nights, I can still remember that I used to enjoy them, and sometimes I even think maybe I should go because it might be good for me. But most of the time, the actual interest or excitement isn’t really there in the way it used to be. It feels kind of far away, like I’m remembering someone else being into those things.\n\nAnd with mentoring, it’s a little different because I still think it’s meaningful, so I don’t want to say I don’t care at all. I do care, at least in my head. But the motivation and the good feeling I used to get from showing up… that’s been really inconsistent, or kind of dulled. So, yeah, I’d say for a lot of the past couple years, I haven’t really been enjoying those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, for most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I know you’re asking “most of the time,” and I get what you mean, but it’s hard for me to separate actual loss of interest from just… not following through, or being tired of the whole process of deciding.\n\nLike, with things I used to enjoy, I can still understand that they’re supposed to be good. I can still remember liking them. Sometimes I’ll even think, “Maybe I should do that again,” but then when it comes time to actually go or make plans, it feels kind of flat. Not always completely empty, just not enough pull to get me moving.\n\nSo I guess I’d say there has been a loss, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it’s been constant or every day. It’s more like the interest comes through really faintly sometimes, and then disappears again. Most of the time it feels dulled or distant, but not totally gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, over the past two years, you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in most of the things you used to enjoy — like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring — for the majority of that time?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can give you a solid answer to that. Like, I know you’re asking if it’s been the majority of the past two years, and I feel like I should be able to look back and say yes or no, but it gets blurry for me.\n\nI can say there have definitely been long stretches where those things didn’t feel the way they used to. Basketball, dance nights, mentoring — I still know what they were supposed to mean to me, but the actual interest or pleasure hasn’t been easy to access. But whether that counts as “most of the things” and “majority of the time,” I don’t know. I don’t really track it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I notice it when I’m supposed to want to do something and I just… don’t feel much. Or I think, “I used to be the kind of person who would care about this more.” But I can’t confidently put a percentage on it. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not the clean answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that over the past two years, you’ve generally lost interest or stopped getting pleasure from most of the things you used to enjoy — not just a few activities, but most of them — for the majority of that time?\n\nPatient: I think… I still get stuck on the “most of them” part. I don’t think I can honestly say “nearly all.” That feels too strong, or at least too precise for what I can actually remember and sort out.

I know you’re trying to get at whether it’s broad, like not just one hobby fading out but almost everything feeling flat most of the time. It’s more like there are times I feel mentally slowed down, but I’m not sure how often or if it’s enough to describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times in the past when you felt down or depressed, and then had a period where you felt significantly better for several months or more before feeling this way again?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve had better days or maybe even a better couple weeks here and there, but I don’t think I’ve had a stretch of several months where I felt significantly better and then it came back again.\n\nIt feels more like this has been pretty continuous for about two years and four months now. It started around when I had to stop taking my community college classes because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule work together. That was kind of the point where things shifted, and since then it hasn’t really gone away in any big, lasting way. Like I said, there are days that are less bad, but I wouldn’t describe it as me being back to normal for months or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about or look forward to, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard for me to say it like, “I don’t care about anything,” because I do care, or at least I know I used to care. But I’ve mostly stopped actually going to things I used to look forward to.\n\nLike basketball—I used to show up for that pretty regularly, and dance nights too. Those were things where even if I was tired, I’d usually still feel like it was worth going. And the church STEM mentoring, I used to really care about that, like helping the younger kids and feeling like I was doing something useful. But lately I just… don’t go. Or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nIt’s also started affecting work, which makes me feel embarrassed to say. I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve been missing shifts, and when I do go, I’m turning in my inspection documentation late. I’m an inspector, so the documentation is part of the job, it’s not like some optional thing. I know that. But it just piles up, and then I avoid it, and then it’s late.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest, or maybe the follow-through on caring. I don’t know if that makes sense, but things I used to be connected to have kind of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you feel restless or unable to sit still, almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything obvious like people telling me I’m talking slower, or that I’m moving differently. I mean, sometimes I feel heavy or kind of like it takes more effort to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as my movements actually being slowed down.\n\nAnd with restlessness… I don’t think I’m pacing all the time or unable to sit still every day. Sometimes I shift around or feel uncomfortable, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking. I guess if someone else watched me maybe they’d notice something I don’t, but from my side, I wouldn’t confidently say yes to either of those almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby you used to enjoy before this low mood started—something you looked forward to—and tell me how you feel about it now? Do you still get pleasure from it, or does it feel like that enjoyment is gone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… basketball is probably the clearest one. I used to really look forward to it. Like, even if I had work or I was tired, I’d still kind of have that feeling of, “Okay, at least I get to go play,” or just be around people and move around and not be in my head so much. Same with dance nights, honestly. I wasn’t amazing or anything, but it was fun, and it felt like something that was mine.\n\nNow it’s more like… I’ll think about going, and then it just feels like too much effort, or like I already know I’m not going to enjoy it the same way, so I don’t go. I’ve mostly stopped showing up for basketball and dance nights. And the church STEM mentoring too—that one makes me feel really guilty, because I did care about it. I liked helping the kids and feeling useful. But now I keep missing it or putting it off, and then I feel worse because I didn’t go.\n\nIt’s not that I hate those things now. It’s more like the part of me that used to get something out of them is kind of muted or gone. I can remember enjoying them, but actually going and feeling that enjoyment now doesn’t really happen much. And it’s spilled into work too, which is embarrassing—I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve been missing shifts and turning in my inspection documentation late. So it’s not just hobbies being less fun, it’s like I’m not following through on things I used to be able to do.\n\nDoctor: Can you say that your low mood has been present most of the day, nearly every day, for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I can say that. It’s been about two years and four months now, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started around when I had to stop taking my community-college classes because of money and my work schedule. I remember that pretty clearly because it felt like this big shift, like I was trying to make it work and then I just couldn’t, and after that my mood kind of settled into this low place and never really went back to normal. It’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but most days, for most of the day, it’s there. \n\nSo yes—at least two years. A little over two years, actually.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely stopped getting any pleasure or satisfaction from activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, or does it feel like there’s still some part of you that knows they’re good, even if you don’t follow through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely gone, like zero pleasure across the board. That feels too absolute, maybe. It’s more like… I can still recognize that those things matter to me, or mattered to me. Like with basketball, I know on some level it’s good for me, and I know I used to like the feeling of playing and being around people. With mentoring, I still know it’s a good thing, and I still like the idea of being helpful.\n\nBut the actual feeling of looking forward to it, or feeling satisfied afterward, is just really dulled down. Sometimes I’ll tell myself, “You should go, you might feel better,” but it doesn’t really connect emotionally. It’s like I know it in my head but I don’t feel it in my body, if that makes sense.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped getting any pleasure ever. It’s more that the pleasure is faint or unreliable, and most of the time the effort of doing it feels bigger than whatever good feeling I might get out of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, for most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think so, but I’m not sure how to measure “most of the time” exactly. It hasn’t been like I woke up one day and suddenly nothing mattered. It’s more like, over time, those things just became harder to feel connected to.\n\nWith basketball or dance nights, I can still remember that I used to enjoy them, and sometimes I even think maybe I should go because it might be good for me. But most of the time, the actual interest or excitement isn’t really there in the way it used to be. It feels kind of far away, like I’m remembering someone else being into those things.\n\nAnd with mentoring, it’s a little different because I still think it’s meaningful, so I don’t want to say I don’t care at all. I do care, at least in my head. But the motivation and the good feeling I used to get from showing up… that’s been really inconsistent, or kind of dulled. So, yeah, I’d say for a lot of the past couple years, I haven’t really been enjoying those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, for most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I know you’re asking “most of the time,” and I get what you mean, but it’s hard for me to separate actual loss of interest from just… not following through, or being tired of the whole process of deciding.\n\nLike, with things I used to enjoy, I can still understand that they’re supposed to be good. I can still remember liking them. Sometimes I’ll even think, “Maybe I should do that again,” but then when it comes time to actually go or make plans, it feels kind of flat. Not always completely empty, just not enough pull to get me moving.\n\nSo I guess I’d say there has been a loss, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it’s been constant or every day. It’s more like the interest comes through really faintly sometimes, and then disappears again. Most of the time it feels dulled or distant, but not totally gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, over the past two years, you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in most of the things you used to enjoy — like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring — for the majority of that time?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can give you a solid answer to that. Like, I know you’re asking if it’s been the majority of the past two years, and I feel like I should be able to look back and say yes or no, but it gets blurry for me.\n\nI can say there have definitely been long stretches where those things didn’t feel the way they used to. Basketball, dance nights, mentoring — I still know what they were supposed to mean to me, but the actual interest or pleasure hasn’t been easy to access. But whether that counts as “most of the things” and “majority of the time,” I don’t know. I don’t really track it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I notice it when I’m supposed to want to do something and I just… don’t feel much. Like I can tell you there are things that don’t feel as good anymore, and there are plenty of times where I feel kind of disconnected from things I used to be more excited about. But when you ask if it’s most things, for the majority of the last two years, I don’t know if I can honestly say that with confidence.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to avoid the question. I just don’t really have a clear mental record of it. Some things I still do because I know they matter, or because I think I should, but I don’t always know if that means I’ve lost pleasure in them or if I’m just not as engaged as I used to be. And then other times I might enjoy something a little, but it feels muted, so I don’t know whether that counts as enjoying it or not.\n\nSo… I guess the most accurate answer is that I’m not sure. It’s just… when you say “almost all of the activities,” I start second-guessing myself because I can still have moments where something is okay, or I can still care about the idea of something, even if actually doing it doesn’t feel very rewarding.\n\nSo if I’m answering carefully, I’d say I’ve had a noticeable loss of interest or pleasure, yes, and it’s been around a lot. I don’t have that clear of a yes in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I hear what you’re asking, and I know you’re trying to make it less absolute than “nearly all,” which does help a little. But I still don’t feel completely sure saying yes to “the majority of activities” for “most of the time.” I wish I had a clearer answer, because I know this is probably important.

What I can say is that a lot of things have felt dulled or harder to care about than they used to. Like, I’ll still do things sometimes, or I’ll think I should want to do them, but the feeling behind it isn’t always there. It’s more muted, or like I’m watching myself go through the motions. But then I get stuck trying to decide if that counts as losing interest or pleasure, because sometimes I might enjoy part of something, or I might feel a little bit of connection to it, just not in the same way.

So… I guess my honest answer is still that I don’t know. There’s definitely been a noticeable change, and it’s affected more than just one or two things, but I can’t confidently say it’s been the majority of the things I used to enjoy for most of the last two years.

I mean, I know I’m hard on myself sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I feel worthless, exactly, or guilty more often than not.\n\nIt’s more like… I can second-guess myself a lot or feel like I should be doing better somehow, but when you say “worthless,” that feels like a really strong word, and I don’t know if that’s what I’m feeling. I’ll feel bad about little things sometimes, like if I didn’t do something the way I thought I should, but I’m not sure it’s this constant guilt that doesn’t make sense logically.\n\nI guess I haven’t really tracked it that way. I mean, I have moments where I’m kind of foggy or I’ll sit there longer than I probably should before doing something, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as not being able to think clearly or concentrate almost every day.\n\nWith decisions, I can overthink things sometimes, but I don’t know that it feels like a separate problem, if that makes sense. Like, if I’m picking something or trying to decide what to do next, sometimes I just feel stuck, but I don’t really know whether that’s because I can’t decide or because I just don’t feel very motivated in that moment.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a way where I’d confidently say yes, it’s hard to concentrate or make decisions almost every day. It’s more like there are times I feel mentally slowed down, but I’m not sure how often or if it’s enough to describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times in the past when you felt down or depressed, and then had a period where you felt significantly better for several months or more before feeling this way again?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve had better days or maybe even a better couple weeks here and there, but I don’t think I’ve had a stretch of several months where I felt significantly better and then it came back again.\n\nIt feels more like this has been pretty continuous for about two years and four months now. It started around when I had to stop taking my community college classes because I couldn’t make the money and my work schedule work together. That was kind of the point where things shifted, and since then it hasn’t really gone away in any big, lasting way. Like I said, there are days that are less bad, but I wouldn’t describe it as me being back to normal for months or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to care about or look forward to, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard for me to say it like, “I don’t care about anything,” because I do care, or at least I know I used to care. Those were things where even if I was tired, I’d usually still feel like it was worth going. And the church STEM mentoring, I used to really care about that, like helping the younger kids and feeling like I was doing something useful. But lately I just… don’t go. Or I tell myself I’ll go next time, and then I don’t.\n\nIt’s also started affecting work, which makes me feel embarrassed to say. I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve been missing shifts, and when I do go, I’m turning in my inspection documentation late. I’m an inspector, so the documentation is part of the job, it’s not like some optional thing. I know that. But it just piles up, and then I avoid it, and then it’s late.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest, or maybe the follow-through on caring. I don’t know if that makes sense, but things I used to be connected to have kind of fallen away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you feel restless or unable to sit still, almost every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything obvious like people telling me I’m talking slower, or that I’m moving differently. I mean, sometimes I feel heavy or kind of like it takes more effort to get going, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as my movements actually being slowed down.\n\nAnd with restlessness… I don’t think I’m pacing all the time or unable to sit still every day. Sometimes I shift around or feel uncomfortable, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking. I guess if someone else watched me maybe they’d notice something I don’t, but from my side, I wouldn’t confidently say yes to either of those almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific activity or hobby you used to enjoy before this low mood started—something you looked forward to—and tell me how you feel about it now? Do you still get pleasure from it, or does it feel like that enjoyment is gone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… basketball is probably the clearest one. I used to really look forward to it. Like, even if I had work or I was tired, I’d still kind of have that feeling of, “Okay, at least I get to go play,” or just be around people and move around and not be in my head so much. Same with dance nights, honestly. I wasn’t amazing or anything, but it was fun, and it felt like something that was mine.\n\nNow it’s more like… I’ll think about going, and then it just feels like too much effort, or like I already know I’m not going to enjoy it the same way, so I don’t go. I’ve mostly stopped showing up for basketball and dance nights. And the church STEM mentoring too—that one makes me feel really guilty, because I did care about it. I liked helping the kids and feeling useful. But now I keep missing it or putting it off, and then I feel worse because I didn’t go.\n\nIt’s not that I hate those things now. It’s more like the part of me that used to get something out of them is kind of muted or gone. I can remember enjoying them, but actually going and feeling that enjoyment now doesn’t really happen much. And it’s spilled into work too, which is embarrassing—I’ve gotten written warnings because I’ve been missing shifts and turning in my inspection documentation late. So it’s not just hobbies being less fun, it’s like I’m not following through on things I used to be able to do.\n\nDoctor: Can you say that your low mood has been present most of the day, nearly every day, for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I can say that. It’s been about two years and four months now, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started around when I had to stop taking my community-college classes because of money and my work schedule. I remember that pretty clearly because it felt like this big shift, like I was trying to make it work and then I just couldn’t, and after that my mood kind of settled into this low place and never really went back to normal. It’s not like every single minute is exactly the same, but most days, for most of the day, it’s there. \n\nSo yes—at least two years. A little over two years, actually.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely stopped getting any pleasure or satisfaction from activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, or does it feel like there’s still some part of you that knows they’re good, even if you don’t follow through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s completely gone, like zero pleasure across the board. That feels too absolute, maybe. It’s more like… I can still recognize that those things matter to me, or mattered to me. Like with basketball, I know on some level it’s good for me, and I know I used to like the feeling of playing and being around people. With mentoring, I still know it’s a good thing, and I still like the idea of being helpful.\n\nBut the actual feeling of looking forward to it, or feeling satisfied afterward, is just really dulled down. Sometimes I’ll tell myself, “You should go, you might feel better,” but it doesn’t really connect emotionally. It’s like I know it in my head but I don’t feel it in my body, if that makes sense.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve completely stopped getting any pleasure ever. It’s more that the pleasure is faint or unreliable, and most of the time the effort of doing it feels bigger than whatever good feeling I might get out of it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, for most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think so, but I’m not sure how to measure “most of the time” exactly. It hasn’t been like I woke up one day and suddenly nothing mattered. It’s more like, over time, those things just became harder to feel connected to.\n\nWith basketball or dance nights, I can still remember that I used to enjoy them, and sometimes I even think maybe I should go because it might be good for me. But most of the time, the actual interest or excitement isn’t really there in the way it used to be. It feels kind of far away, like I’m remembering someone else being into those things.\n\nAnd with mentoring, it’s a little different because I still think it’s meaningful, so I don’t want to say I don’t care at all. I do care, at least in my head. But the motivation and the good feeling I used to get from showing up… that’s been really inconsistent, or kind of dulled. So, yeah, I’d say for a lot of the past couple years, I haven’t really been enjoying those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring, for most of the time over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I know you’re asking “most of the time,” and I get what you mean, but it’s hard for me to separate actual loss of interest from just… not following through, or being tired of the whole process of deciding.\n\nLike, with things I used to enjoy, I can still understand that they’re supposed to be good. I can still remember liking them. Sometimes I’ll even think, “Maybe I should do that again,” but then when it comes time to actually go or make plans, it feels kind of flat. Not always completely empty, just not enough pull to get me moving.\n\nSo I guess I’d say there has been a loss, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it’s been constant or every day. It’s more like the interest comes through really faintly sometimes, and then disappears again. Most of the time it feels dulled or distant, but not totally gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, over the past two years, you’ve experienced a loss of pleasure or interest in most of the things you used to enjoy — like basketball, dance nights, or mentoring — for the majority of that time?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can give you a solid answer to that. Like, I know you’re asking if it’s been the majority of the past two years, and I feel like I should be able to look back and say yes or no, but it gets blurry for me.\n\nI can say there have definitely been long stretches where those things didn’t feel the way they used to. Basketball, dance nights, mentoring — I still know what they were supposed to mean to me, but the actual interest or pleasure hasn’t been easy to access. But whether that counts as “most of the things” and “majority of the time,” I don’t know. I don’t really track it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I notice it when I’m supposed to want to do something and I just… don’t feel much. Or I think, “I used to be the kind of person who would care about this more.” But I can’t confidently put a percentage on it. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not the clean answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that over the past two years, you’ve generally lost interest or stopped getting pleasure from most of the things you used to enjoy — not just a few activities, but most of them — for the majority of that time?\n\nPatient: I think… I still get stuck on the “most of them” part. Like I can tell you there are things that don’t feel as good anymore, and there are plenty of times where I feel kind of disconnected from things I used to be more excited about. But when you ask if it’s most things, for the majority of the last two years, I don’t know if I can honestly say that with confidence.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to avoid the question. I just don’t really have a clear mental record of it. Some things I still do because I know they matter, or because I think I should, but I don’t always know if that means I’ve lost pleasure in them or if I’m just not as engaged as I used to be. And then other times I might enjoy something a little, but it feels muted, so I don’t know whether that counts as enjoying it or not.\n\nSo… I guess the most accurate answer is that I’m not sure. I’ve noticed a change, definitely, but I can’t clearly say it’s been most activities for most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that over the past two years, you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in almost all of the activities you used to enjoy — not just a few, but nearly all of them — most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can honestly say “nearly all.” That feels too strong, or at least too precise for what I can actually remember and sort out.\n\nI know you’re trying to get at whether it’s broad, like not just one hobby fading out but almost everything feeling flat most of the time. It’s just… when you say “almost all of the activities,” I start second-guessing myself because I can still have moments where something is okay, or I can still care about the idea of something, even if actually doing it doesn’t feel very rewarding.\n\nSo if I’m answering carefully, I’d say I’ve had a noticeable loss of interest or pleasure, yes, and it’s been around a lot. But I can’t confidently say it’s been almost all activities, most of the time, for the whole two years. I don’t have that clear of a yes in my head.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that for most of the time over the past two years, you’ve experienced a noticeable loss of pleasure or interest in the majority of the activities you used to enjoy, even if not every single one?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know you’re trying to make it less absolute than “nearly all,” which does help a little. It’s just… when you say “almost all of the activities,” I start second-guessing myself because I can still have moments where something is okay, or I can still care about the idea of something, even if actually doing it doesn’t feel very rewarding.\n\nSo if I’m answering carefully, I’d say I’ve had a noticeable loss of interest or pleasure, yes, and it’s been around a lot. But I can’t confidently say it’s been almost all activities, most of the time, for the whole two years. I don’t have that clear of a yes in my head.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that for most of the time over the past two years, you’ve experienced a noticeable loss of pleasure or interest in the majority of the activities you used to enjoy, even if not every single one?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know you’re trying to make it less absolute than “nearly all,” which does help a little. But I still don’t feel completely sure saying yes to “the majority of activities” for “most of the time.” I wish I had a clearer answer, because I know this is probably important.\n\nWhat I can say is that a lot of things have felt dulled or harder to care about than they used to. Like, I’ll still do things sometimes, or I’ll think I should want to do them, but the feeling behind it isn’t always there. It’s more muted, or like I’m watching myself go through the motions. But then I get stuck trying to decide if that counts as losing interest or pleasure, because sometimes I might enjoy part of something, or I might feel a little bit of connection to it, just not in the same way.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is still that I don’t know. There’s definitely been a noticeable change, and it’s affected more than just one or two things, but I can’t confidently say it’s been the majority of the things I used to enjoy for most of the last two years."
